What brings you in to see me today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 37 / computer_or_information_systems_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Increased_Goal_Activity\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I can’t seem to stop launching projects—work plans, certification schedules, home network upgrades, messages to old contacts—and it gets so intense that I blow past normal limits until my wife or my director has to step in.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, um… I’m here because things have been getting kind of out of hand with how much I’m taking on. Like I’ll start a work plan, then suddenly I’m mapping out certification schedules, then I’m redesigning the whole home network, and then I’m messaging people I haven’t talked to in years because I think maybe there’s some opportunity there.

It’s not just being productive, though. I sort of blow past normal limits, and then my wife or my director has to step in and tell me to stop or slow down. I guess that’s the main reason I came in.

Yeah… I mean, my wife would say yes, probably faster than I would.

It’s not always like I’m buying sports cars or something. Sometimes it’s “work stuff,” you know? Gear, subscriptions, things I convinced myself were necessary because I was going to fix everything or build something better overnight. And then it kind of blends into the bigger mess.

After that migration failed at work, I got put on leave, and at home my wife had to start stepping in more—watching the spending, telling me to stop working at midnight, trying to keep normal conversations from turning into arguments. I hate saying that because it makes me sound like a child.

And I’ve pulled back from other things too—church music, climbing, mentoring people—because I can’t reliably show up or follow through. So… yeah, the spending is part of it, but it’s more like the whole thing spills over everywhere.

I’ll be lying there thinking about all the stuff I can get done, and then I’m already up before the alarm. Which sounds useful, I guess, except it’s not really normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt extremely high or irritable that lasted for several days or longer?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it can last more than just a bad afternoon. Some weeks I get this almost electric feeling, like I’m unstoppable and everything should move at my speed.\n\nBut then the smallest thing can flip it. Like if something takes an extra minute, or my wife asks me a normal question, I’ll just snap, like—why are you trying to slow me down? And I know that sounds ridiculous when I say it sitting here, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these mood swings and periods of high energy?\n\nPatient: I think… about a year and eight months, roughly. Not like I wrote it down on a calendar, but that’s when I started noticing these stretches where I was way more driven, barely sleeping, and feeling like I knew better than everyone.\n\nIt’s weird, because at first I kind of told myself it was just work stress or getting into a productive groove—my job has these crunch periods, and everybody talks about “grinding” like it’s normal. But looking back, yeah, the high-energy, overconfident stuff has been going on for around a year and eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever spent money recklessly or made impulsive purchases during these high-energy periods?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, my wife would say yes, probably faster than I would.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m buying sports cars or something. Sometimes it’s “work stuff,” you know? Gear, subscriptions, things I convinced myself were necessary because I was going to fix everything or build something better overnight. And then it kind of blends into the bigger mess.\n\nAfter that migration failed at work, I got put on leave, and at home my wife had to start stepping in more—watching the spending, telling me to stop working at midnight, trying to keep normal conversations from turning into arguments. I hate saying that because it makes me sound like a child.\n\nAnd I’ve pulled back from other things too—church music, climbing, mentoring people—because I can’t reliably show up or follow through. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sort of… I mean, I do come down from that wired, speeding feeling. There are stretches where I’m not especially “up,” and I’m not really depressed either—just kind of calm, or flat, I guess. Sometimes that lasts a few weeks.

But I don’t know if I’d call it completely normal, because even during those quieter times, I’ll still get stuck on things. Like Teams statuses at work—I know it sounds stupid—but I’ll feel like they’re coded warnings, like people are signaling something without saying it. And sometimes I’ll hear these quick little comments when nobody’s actually there.

So mood-wise, yeah, I can seem more normal between the high-energy periods. But it’s not like everything fully shuts off.

And I know that sounds ridiculous when I say it sitting here, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these mood swings and periods of high energy?\n\nPatient: I think… about a year and eight months, roughly. Not like I wrote it down on a calendar, but that’s when I started noticing these stretches where I was way more driven, barely sleeping, and feeling like I knew better than everyone.\n\nIt’s weird, because at first I kind of told myself it was just work stress or getting into a productive groove—my job has these crunch periods, and everybody talks about “grinding” like it’s normal. But looking back, yeah, the high-energy, overconfident stuff has been going on for around a year and eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever spent money recklessly or made impulsive purchases during these high-energy periods?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, my wife would say yes, probably faster than I would.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m buying sports cars or something. Sometimes it’s “work stuff,” you know? Gear, subscriptions, things I convinced myself were necessary because I was going to fix everything or build something better overnight. And then it kind of blends into the bigger mess.\n\nAfter that migration failed at work, I got put on leave, and at home my wife had to start stepping in more—watching the spending, telling me to stop working at midnight, trying to keep normal conversations from turning into arguments. I hate saying that because it makes me sound like a child.\n\nAnd I’ve pulled back from other things too—church music, climbing, mentoring people—because I can’t reliably show up or follow through. So… yeah, the spending is part of it, but it’s more like the whole thing spills over everywhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you return to a normal state between these high-energy periods?\n\nPatient: Sort of… I mean, I do come down from that wired, speeding feeling. There are stretches where I’m not especially “up,” and I’m not really depressed either—just kind of calm, or flat, I guess. Sometimes that lasts a few weeks.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d call it completely normal, because even during those quieter times, I’ll still get stuck on things. Like Teams statuses at work—I know it sounds stupid—but I’ll feel like they’re coded warnings, like people are signaling something without saying it. Yeah… yeah, I have.

It’s not always dramatic, like I’m crying all day or anything. It’s more like I wake up and I already feel like this failed version of myself, like I’m behind before the day even starts. Heavy, flat. And then my wife will ask something normal—like what I want for dinner, or if I paid a bill—and it feels like too much to even answer, which makes no sense because it’s just a question.

I don’t know… sometimes I’ll sit there thinking I should be able to handle basic things. I manage systems for a living, or I’m supposed to, and then at home I can’t even have a simple conversation without feeling buried by it. So yes, there are definitely periods where I feel really down.

I’ll be trying to finish a report or close out a ticket, and then my phone buzzes, or the fan makes this little clicking sound, or someone just walks past my office door, and my attention just snaps over there.\n\nAnd then somehow I’m not doing the thing anymore. I’ll be checking the message, or thinking about whether the fan needs replacing—actually I had this one fan in my office that kept doing that, and I spent way too long looking up quieter ones—anyway, yeah, it’s hard to stay with one thing for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that your mood changes rapidly and unexpectedly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty often. It can come on really fast, like I’ll be feeling… I don’t know, charged up, almost electric, like I can handle everything and nothing can touch me.\n\nAnd then something small happens—my wife asks a simple question, or there’s a little delay with something—and I just snap. In the moment it feels like people are slowing me down on purpose, even if later I can see that maybe they weren’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt extremely high or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah. There are stretches where I’ll sleep maybe three hours, and then I’m just… up. Like wide awake at 3 or 4 in the morning, not groggy, not thinking, “Oh no, I need more sleep.”\n\nIt feels more like someone gave me extra hours in the day. I’ll be lying there thinking about all the stuff I can get done, and then I’m already up before the alarm. Which sounds useful, I guess, except it’s not really normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt extremely high or irritable that lasted for several days or longer?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it can last more than just a bad afternoon. Some weeks I get this almost electric feeling, like I’m unstoppable and everything should move at my speed.\n\nBut then the smallest thing can flip it. Like if something takes an extra minute, or my wife asks me a normal question, I’ll just snap, like—why are you trying to slow me down? And I know that sounds ridiculous when I say it sitting here, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these mood swings and periods of high energy?\n\nPatient: I think… about a year and eight months, roughly. Not like I wrote it down on a calendar, but that’s when I started noticing these stretches where I was way more driven, barely sleeping, and feeling like I knew better than everyone.\n\nIt’s weird, because at first I kind of told myself it was just work stress or getting into a productive groove—my job has these crunch periods, and everybody talks about “grinding” like it’s normal. But looking back, yeah, the high-energy, overconfident stuff has been going on for around a year and eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever spent money recklessly or made impulsive purchases during these high-energy periods?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, my wife would say yes, probably faster than I would.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m buying sports cars or something. Sometimes it’s “work stuff,” you know? Gear, subscriptions, things I convinced myself were necessary because I was going to fix everything or build something better overnight. And then it kind of blends into the bigger mess.\n\nAfter that migration failed at work, I got put on leave, and at home my wife had to start stepping in more—watching the spending, telling me to stop working at midnight, trying to keep normal conversations from turning into arguments. I hate saying that because it makes me sound like a child.\n\nAnd I’ve pulled back from other things too—church music, climbing, mentoring people—because I can’t reliably show up or follow through. So… yeah, the spending is part of it, but it’s more like the whole thing spills over everywhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you return to a normal state between these high-energy periods?\n\nPatient: Sort of… I mean, I do come down from that wired, speeding feeling. There are stretches where I’m not especially “up,” and I’m not really depressed either—just kind of calm, or flat, I guess. Sometimes that lasts a few weeks.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d call it completely normal, because even during those quieter times, I’ll still get stuck on things. Like Teams statuses at work—I know it sounds stupid—but I’ll feel like they’re coded warnings, like people are signaling something without saying it. And sometimes I’ll hear these quick little comments when nobody’s actually there.\n\nSo mood-wise, yeah, I can seem more normal between the high-energy periods. But it’s not like everything fully shuts off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt really down or depressed for a period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic, like I’m crying all day or anything. It’s more like I wake up and I already feel like this failed version of myself, like I’m behind before the day even starts. Heavy, flat. And then my wife will ask something normal—like what I want for dinner, or if I paid a bill—and it feels like too much to even answer, which makes no sense because it’s just a question.\n\nI don’t know… sometimes I’ll sit there thinking I should be able to handle basic things. I manage systems for a living, or I’m supposed to, and then at home I can’t even have a simple conversation without feeling buried by it. So yes, there are definitely periods where I feel really down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, very much. Especially when I’m revved up.

Like, I’ll be trying to finish a report or respond to a ticket, and if my phone buzzes—even once—I’m gone. Or the fan in my office will click weird and suddenly I’m listening to that instead of reading what’s in front of me. Someone walks past my door and I’ll look up, and then somehow I’m opening another tab, checking something else, and the thing I was supposed to finish is just… abandoned.

It’s frustrating because I know it looks like I’m being careless, but it feels more like my attention just gets yanked away before I can stop it.

In the moment it feels like people are slowing me down on purpose, even if later I can see that maybe they weren’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt extremely high or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah. There are stretches where I’ll sleep maybe three hours, and then I’m just… up. I’ll be lying there thinking about all the stuff I can get done, and then I’m already up before the alarm. Which sounds useful, I guess, except it’s not really normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt extremely high or irritable that lasted for several days or longer?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it can last more than just a bad afternoon. Some weeks I get this almost electric feeling, like I’m unstoppable and everything should move at my speed.\n\nBut then the smallest thing can flip it. Not like I wrote it down on a calendar, but that’s when I started noticing these stretches where I was way more driven, barely sleeping, and feeling like I knew better than everyone.\n\nIt’s weird, because at first I kind of told myself it was just work stress or getting into a productive groove—my job has these crunch periods, and everybody talks about “grinding” like it’s normal. But looking back, yeah, the high-energy, overconfident stuff has been going on for around a year and eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever spent money recklessly or made impulsive purchases during these high-energy periods?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, my wife would say yes, probably faster than I would.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m buying sports cars or something. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It comes and goes, I think. It’s not like I’m exactly the same level all the time.

There are weeks where I feel almost electric, like I can do anything and I don’t understand why everyone else is moving so slowly. And then—this is the part I don’t like—something really small, like a delay on a project or my wife just asking me a normal question, can make me snap. In my head it feels like people are slowing me down on purpose.

Which, I mean, I know that sounds kind of ridiculous sitting here saying it. It’s not like she’s doing anything wrong. But when I’m in that state, it doesn’t feel small. It feels like everything is in my way.

I’ll be checking the message, or thinking about whether the fan needs replacing—actually I had this one fan in my office that kept doing that, and I spent way too long looking up quieter ones—anyway, yeah, it’s hard to stay with one thing for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that your mood changes rapidly and unexpectedly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty often. It can come on really fast, like I’ll be feeling… I don’t know, charged up, almost electric, like I can handle everything and nothing can touch me.\n\nAnd then something small happens—my wife asks a simple question, or there’s a little delay with something—and I just snap. In the moment it feels like people are slowing me down on purpose, even if later I can see that maybe they weren’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt extremely high or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah. There are stretches where I’ll sleep maybe three hours, and then I’m just… up. Like wide awake at 3 or 4 in the morning, not groggy, not thinking, “Oh no, I need more sleep.”\n\nIt feels more like someone gave me extra hours in the day. I’ll be lying there thinking about all the stuff I can get done, and then I’m already up before the alarm. Which sounds useful, I guess, except it’s not really normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt extremely high or irritable that lasted for several days or longer?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it can last more than just a bad afternoon. Some weeks I get this almost electric feeling, like I’m unstoppable and everything should move at my speed.\n\nBut then the smallest thing can flip it. And I know that sounds ridiculous when I say it sitting here, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these mood swings and periods of high energy?\n\nPatient: I think… about a year and eight months, roughly. Not like I wrote it down on a calendar, but that’s when I started noticing these stretches where I was way more driven, barely sleeping, and feeling like I knew better than everyone.\n\nIt’s weird, because at first I kind of told myself it was just work stress or getting into a productive groove—my job has these crunch periods, and everybody talks about “grinding” like it’s normal. But looking back, yeah, the high-energy, overconfident stuff has been going on for around a year and eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever spent money recklessly or made impulsive purchases during these high-energy periods?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, my wife would say yes, probably faster than I would.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m buying sports cars or something. Sometimes that lasts a few weeks.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d call it completely normal, because even during those quieter times, I’ll still get stuck on things. Like Teams statuses at work—I know it sounds stupid—but I’ll feel like they’re coded warnings, like people are signaling something without saying it. And sometimes I’ll hear these quick little comments when nobody’s actually there.\n\nSo mood-wise, yeah, I can seem more normal between the high-energy periods. Like if my wife asks one question or something takes five minutes longer than I expected, I can snap at it like people are deliberately trying to slow me down. Even if, later, I know that’s probably not what was happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I’ll be trying to finish a report or close out a ticket, and then my phone buzzes, or the fan makes this little clicking sound, or someone just walks past my office door, and my attention just snaps over there.\n\nAnd then somehow I’m not doing the thing anymore. I’ll be checking the message, or thinking about whether the fan needs replacing—actually I had this one fan in my office that kept doing that, and I spent way too long looking up quieter ones—anyway, yeah, it’s hard to stay with one thing for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that your mood changes rapidly and unexpectedly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty often. It can come on really fast, like I’ll be feeling… I don’t know, charged up, almost electric, like I can handle everything and nothing can touch me.\n\nAnd then something small happens—my wife asks a simple question, or there’s a little delay with something—and I just snap. In the moment it feels like people are slowing me down on purpose, even if later I can see that maybe they weren’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt extremely high or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah. There are stretches where I’ll sleep maybe three hours, and then I’m just… up. Like wide awake at 3 or 4 in the morning, not groggy, not thinking, “Oh no, I need more sleep.”\n\nIt feels more like someone gave me extra hours in the day. I’ll be lying there thinking about all the stuff I can get done, and then I’m already up before the alarm. Which sounds useful, I guess, except it’s not really normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt extremely high or irritable that lasted for several days or longer?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it can last more than just a bad afternoon. Some weeks I get this almost electric feeling, like I’m unstoppable and everything should move at my speed.\n\nBut then the smallest thing can flip it. Like if something takes an extra minute, or my wife asks me a normal question, I’ll just snap, like—why are you trying to slow me down? And I know that sounds ridiculous when I say it sitting here, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these mood swings and periods of high energy?\n\nPatient: I think… about a year and eight months, roughly. Not like I wrote it down on a calendar, but that’s when I started noticing these stretches where I was way more driven, barely sleeping, and feeling like I knew better than everyone.\n\nIt’s weird, because at first I kind of told myself it was just work stress or getting into a productive groove—my job has these crunch periods, and everybody talks about “grinding” like it’s normal. But looking back, yeah, the high-energy, overconfident stuff has been going on for around a year and eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever spent money recklessly or made impulsive purchases during these high-energy periods?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, my wife would say yes, probably faster than I would.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m buying sports cars or something. Sometimes it’s “work stuff,” you know? Gear, subscriptions, things I convinced myself were necessary because I was going to fix everything or build something better overnight. And then it kind of blends into the bigger mess.\n\nAfter that migration failed at work, I got put on leave, and at home my wife had to start stepping in more—watching the spending, telling me to stop working at midnight, trying to keep normal conversations from turning into arguments. I hate saying that because it makes me sound like a child.\n\nAnd I’ve pulled back from other things too—church music, climbing, mentoring people—because I can’t reliably show up or follow through. So… yeah, the spending is part of it, but it’s more like the whole thing spills over everywhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you return to a normal state between these high-energy periods?\n\nPatient: Sort of… I mean, I do come down from that wired, speeding feeling. There are stretches where I’m not especially “up,” and I’m not really depressed either—just kind of calm, or flat, I guess. Sometimes that lasts a few weeks.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d call it completely normal, because even during those quieter times, I’ll still get stuck on things. Like Teams statuses at work—I know it sounds stupid—but I’ll feel like they’re coded warnings, like people are signaling something without saying it. And sometimes I’ll hear these quick little comments when nobody’s actually there.\n\nSo mood-wise, yeah, I can seem more normal between the high-energy periods. But it’s not like everything fully shuts off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt really down or depressed for a period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic, like I’m crying all day or anything. It’s more like I wake up and I already feel like this failed version of myself, like I’m behind before the day even starts. Heavy, flat. And then my wife will ask something normal—like what I want for dinner, or if I paid a bill—and it feels like too much to even answer, which makes no sense because it’s just a question.\n\nI don’t know… sometimes I’ll sit there thinking I should be able to handle basic things. I manage systems for a living, or I’m supposed to, and then at home I can’t even have a simple conversation without feeling buried by it. So yes, there are definitely periods where I feel really down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things that wouldn't normally distract other people?\n\nPatient: Yeah, very much. Especially when I’m revved up.\n\nLike, I’ll be trying to finish a report or respond to a ticket, and if my phone buzzes—even once—I’m gone. Or the fan in my office will click weird and suddenly I’m listening to that instead of reading what’s in front of me. Someone walks past my door and I’ll look up, and then somehow I’m opening another tab, checking something else, and the thing I was supposed to finish is just… abandoned.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I know it looks like I’m being careless, but it feels more like my attention just gets yanked away before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely high or irritable that lasted for at least a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There are some weeks where I feel almost electric, like I’m unstoppable and everything should move at my speed.\n\nAnd then it’s weird, because it doesn’t take much to flip it into irritation. Like if there’s a tiny delay, or my wife asks me a simple question, I’ll suddenly feel like she’s slowing me down on purpose. Which, I know, that sounds unfair when I say it out loud. But in the moment it feels like everybody is in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these high-energy periods come and go, or is it more like your mood and energy level are always fluctuating?\n\nPatient: It comes and goes, I think. It’s not like I’m exactly the same level all the time.\n\nThere are weeks where I feel almost electric, like I can do anything and I don’t understand why everyone else is moving so slowly. And then—this is the part I don’t like—something really small, like a delay on a project or my wife just asking me a normal question, can make me snap. In my head it feels like people are slowing me down on purpose.\n\nWhich, I mean, I know that sounds kind of ridiculous sitting here saying it. It’s not like she’s doing anything wrong. But when I’m in that state, it doesn’t feel small. It feels like everything is in my way.\n\nDoctor: Do you consider these high-energy periods to be separate episodes, or do they blend together over time?\n\nPatient: They feel more like separate stretches, I guess—not one constant thing. Like I’ll have these runs where I’m unusually driven and sleeping less, and I feel way too sure of myself.\n\nIt’s been happening… maybe for about a year and eight months now? I remember trying to pin it down because it started affecting work, and then I got distracted thinking it was just the job getting more intense, because in tech everything is always “urgent,” you know? But no, it’s not just the job.\n\nSo yeah, they come in stretches, but over time it’s all kind of started to blur together a little.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always coming down from one high-energy period just to go into another, or are there clear breaks in between?\n\nPatient: There are breaks, I think… but sometimes it feels like I’m just cycling through them, like I finally come down and then some new push starts up again.\n\nThe bigger thing is, those unusually driven stretches—the not sleeping much, feeling overconfident, like I’m operating at a higher speed—those have been part of my life for… about a year and eight months now, roughly. I remember trying to count it out and then getting sidetracked because work has its own crazy rhythm, and it’s hard to tell what’s me and what’s just the job. But it’s been around that long.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d call it completely normal. Like, there are times I’m not as revved up, but then some weeks I feel almost… electric, like I’m unstoppable, and everything is moving too slow around me.

And then it’s weird, because it’s not just “happy” or productive. A tiny delay at work, or my wife asking me a totally normal question, can make me snap. In the moment it feels like people are slowing me down on purpose, even though later I can see that doesn’t really make sense.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I’m here because things have been getting kind of out of hand with how much I’m taking on. Like I’ll start a work plan, then suddenly I’m mapping out certification schedules, then I’m redesigning the whole home network, and then I’m messaging people I haven’t talked to in years because I think maybe there’s some opportunity there.\n\nIt’s not just being productive, though. I sort of blow past normal limits, and then my wife or my director has to step in and tell me to stop or slow down. I guess that’s the main reason I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually happy or upbeat lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, some weeks I do. It’s like I feel almost electric, like I can push through anything and everything is lining up, and I get this sense that I’m unstoppable.\n\nBut then it flips weirdly fast. Like if my wife asks one question or something takes five minutes longer than I expected, I can snap at it like people are deliberately trying to slow me down. Even if, later, I know that’s probably not what was happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I’ll be trying to finish a report or close out a ticket, and then my phone buzzes, or the fan makes this little clicking sound, or someone just walks past my office door, and my attention just snaps over there.\n\nAnd then somehow I’m not doing the thing anymore. I’ll be checking the message, or thinking about whether the fan needs replacing—actually I had this one fan in my office that kept doing that, and I spent way too long looking up quieter ones—anyway, yeah, it’s hard to stay with one thing for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that your mood changes rapidly and unexpectedly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty often. It can come on really fast, like I’ll be feeling… I don’t know, charged up, almost electric, like I can handle everything and nothing can touch me.\n\nAnd then something small happens—my wife asks a simple question, or there’s a little delay with something—and I just snap. In the moment it feels like people are slowing me down on purpose, even if later I can see that maybe they weren’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt extremely high or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah. There are stretches where I’ll sleep maybe three hours, and then I’m just… up. Like wide awake at 3 or 4 in the morning, not groggy, not thinking, “Oh no, I need more sleep.”\n\nIt feels more like someone gave me extra hours in the day. I’ll be lying there thinking about all the stuff I can get done, and then I’m already up before the alarm. Which sounds useful, I guess, except it’s not really normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt extremely high or irritable that lasted for several days or longer?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it can last more than just a bad afternoon. Some weeks I get this almost electric feeling, like I’m unstoppable and everything should move at my speed.\n\nBut then the smallest thing can flip it. Like if something takes an extra minute, or my wife asks me a normal question, I’ll just snap, like—why are you trying to slow me down? And I know that sounds ridiculous when I say it sitting here, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these mood swings and periods of high energy?\n\nPatient: I think… about a year and eight months, roughly. Not like I wrote it down on a calendar, but that’s when I started noticing these stretches where I was way more driven, barely sleeping, and feeling like I knew better than everyone.\n\nIt’s weird, because at first I kind of told myself it was just work stress or getting into a productive groove—my job has these crunch periods, and everybody talks about “grinding” like it’s normal. But looking back, yeah, the high-energy, overconfident stuff has been going on for around a year and eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever spent money recklessly or made impulsive purchases during these high-energy periods?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, my wife would say yes, probably faster than I would.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m buying sports cars or something. Sometimes it’s “work stuff,” you know? Gear, subscriptions, things I convinced myself were necessary because I was going to fix everything or build something better overnight. And then it kind of blends into the bigger mess.\n\nAfter that migration failed at work, I got put on leave, and at home my wife had to start stepping in more—watching the spending, telling me to stop working at midnight, trying to keep normal conversations from turning into arguments. I hate saying that because it makes me sound like a child.\n\nAnd I’ve pulled back from other things too—church music, climbing, mentoring people—because I can’t reliably show up or follow through. So… yeah, the spending is part of it, but it’s more like the whole thing spills over everywhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you return to a normal state between these high-energy periods?\n\nPatient: Sort of… I mean, I do come down from that wired, speeding feeling. There are stretches where I’m not especially “up,” and I’m not really depressed either—just kind of calm, or flat, I guess. Sometimes that lasts a few weeks.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d call it completely normal, because even during those quieter times, I’ll still get stuck on things. Like Teams statuses at work—I know it sounds stupid—but I’ll feel like they’re coded warnings, like people are signaling something without saying it. And sometimes I’ll hear these quick little comments when nobody’s actually there.\n\nSo mood-wise, yeah, I can seem more normal between the high-energy periods. But it’s not like everything fully shuts off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt really down or depressed for a period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic, like I’m crying all day or anything. It’s more like I wake up and I already feel like this failed version of myself, like I’m behind before the day even starts. Heavy, flat. And then my wife will ask something normal—like what I want for dinner, or if I paid a bill—and it feels like too much to even answer, which makes no sense because it’s just a question.\n\nI don’t know… sometimes I’ll sit there thinking I should be able to handle basic things. I manage systems for a living, or I’m supposed to, and then at home I can’t even have a simple conversation without feeling buried by it. So yes, there are definitely periods where I feel really down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things that wouldn't normally distract other people?\n\nPatient: Yeah, very much. Especially when I’m revved up.\n\nLike, I’ll be trying to finish a report or respond to a ticket, and if my phone buzzes—even once—I’m gone. Or the fan in my office will click weird and suddenly I’m listening to that instead of reading what’s in front of me. Someone walks past my door and I’ll look up, and then somehow I’m opening another tab, checking something else, and the thing I was supposed to finish is just… abandoned.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I know it looks like I’m being careless, but it feels more like my attention just gets yanked away before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely high or irritable that lasted for at least a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There are some weeks where I feel almost electric, like I’m unstoppable and everything should move at my speed.\n\nAnd then it’s weird, because it doesn’t take much to flip it into irritation. Like if there’s a tiny delay, or my wife asks me a simple question, I’ll suddenly feel like she’s slowing me down on purpose. Which, I know, that sounds unfair when I say it out loud. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
They kind of blend together, I think. I can point to some stretches that felt like they started with a project or a deadline, but looking back, the high-energy, pressured times have taken up more of the last couple years than the quieter periods.

Not like I wrote it down on a calendar, but that’s when I started noticing these stretches where I was way more driven, barely sleeping, and feeling like I knew better than everyone.\n\nIt’s weird, because at first I kind of told myself it was just work stress or getting into a productive groove—my job has these crunch periods, and everybody talks about “grinding” like it’s normal. But looking back, yeah, the high-energy, overconfident stuff has been going on for around a year and eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever spent money recklessly or made impulsive purchases during these high-energy periods?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, my wife would say yes, probably faster than I would.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m buying sports cars or something. I hate saying that because it makes me sound like a child.\n\nAnd I’ve pulled back from other things too—church music, climbing, mentoring people—because I can’t reliably show up or follow through. So… yeah, the spending is part of it, but it’s more like the whole thing spills over everywhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you return to a normal state between these high-energy periods?\n\nPatient: Sort of… I mean, I do come down from that wired, speeding feeling. There are stretches where I’m not especially “up,” and I’m not really depressed either—just kind of calm, or flat, I guess. Sometimes that lasts a few weeks.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d call it completely normal, because even during those quieter times, I’ll still get stuck on things. Like Teams statuses at work—I know it sounds stupid—but I’ll feel like they’re coded warnings, like people are signaling something without saying it. And sometimes I’ll hear these quick little comments when nobody’s actually there.\n\nSo mood-wise, yeah, I can seem more normal between the high-energy periods. But it’s not like everything fully shuts off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt really down or depressed for a period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic, like I’m crying all day or anything. It’s more like I wake up and I already feel like this failed version of myself, like I’m behind before the day even starts. Heavy, flat. And then my wife will ask something normal—like what I want for dinner, or if I paid a bill—and it feels like too much to even answer, which makes no sense because it’s just a question.\n\nI don’t know… sometimes I’ll sit there thinking I should be able to handle basic things. I manage systems for a living, or I’m supposed to, and then at home I can’t even have a simple conversation without feeling buried by it. Someone walks past my door and I’ll look up, and then somehow I’m opening another tab, checking something else, and the thing I was supposed to finish is just… abandoned.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I know it looks like I’m being careless, but it feels more like my attention just gets yanked away before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely high or irritable that lasted for at least a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. In the moment it feels like people are slowing me down on purpose, even if later I can see that maybe they weren’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt extremely high or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah. There are stretches where I’ll sleep maybe three hours, and then I’m just… up. Like wide awake at 3 or 4 in the morning, not groggy, not thinking, “Oh no, I need more sleep.”\n\nIt feels more like someone gave me extra hours in the day. I’ll be lying there thinking about all the stuff I can get done, and then I’m already up before the alarm. Which sounds useful, I guess, except it’s not really normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt extremely high or irritable that lasted for several days or longer?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it can last more than just a bad afternoon. Some weeks I get this almost electric feeling, like I’m unstoppable and everything should move at my speed.\n\nBut then the smallest thing can flip it. Like if something takes an extra minute, or my wife asks me a normal question, I’ll just snap, like—why are you trying to slow me down? And I know that sounds ridiculous when I say it sitting here, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these mood swings and periods of high energy?\n\nPatient: I think… about a year and eight months, roughly. Not like I wrote it down on a calendar, but that’s when I started noticing these stretches where I was way more driven, barely sleeping, and feeling like I knew better than everyone.\n\nIt’s weird, because at first I kind of told myself it was just work stress or getting into a productive groove—my job has these crunch periods, and everybody talks about “grinding” like it’s normal. But looking back, yeah, the high-energy, overconfident stuff has been going on for around a year and eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever spent money recklessly or made impulsive purchases during these high-energy periods?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, my wife would say yes, probably faster than I would.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m buying sports cars or something. Sometimes it’s “work stuff,” you know? Gear, subscriptions, things I convinced myself were necessary because I was going to fix everything or build something better overnight. And then it kind of blends into the bigger mess.\n\nAfter that migration failed at work, I got put on leave, and at home my wife had to start stepping in more—watching the spending, telling me to stop working at midnight, trying to keep normal conversations from turning into arguments. I hate saying that because it makes me sound like a child.\n\nAnd I’ve pulled back from other things too—church music, climbing, mentoring people—because I can’t reliably show up or follow through. So… yeah, the spending is part of it, but it’s more like the whole thing spills over everywhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you return to a normal state between these high-energy periods?\n\nPatient: Sort of… I mean, I do come down from that wired, speeding feeling. There are stretches where I’m not especially “up,” and I’m not really depressed either—just kind of calm, or flat, I guess. Sometimes that lasts a few weeks.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d call it completely normal, because even during those quieter times, I’ll still get stuck on things. Like Teams statuses at work—I know it sounds stupid—but I’ll feel like they’re coded warnings, like people are signaling something without saying it. And sometimes I’ll hear these quick little comments when nobody’s actually there.\n\nSo mood-wise, yeah, I can seem more normal between the high-energy periods. But it’s not like everything fully shuts off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt really down or depressed for a period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic, like I’m crying all day or anything. It’s more like I wake up and I already feel like this failed version of myself, like I’m behind before the day even starts. Heavy, flat. And then my wife will ask something normal—like what I want for dinner, or if I paid a bill—and it feels like too much to even answer, which makes no sense because it’s just a question.\n\nI don’t know… sometimes I’ll sit there thinking I should be able to handle basic things. I manage systems for a living, or I’m supposed to, and then at home I can’t even have a simple conversation without feeling buried by it. So yes, there are definitely periods where I feel really down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things that wouldn't normally distract other people?\n\nPatient: Yeah, very much. Especially when I’m revved up.\n\nLike, I’ll be trying to finish a report or respond to a ticket, and if my phone buzzes—even once—I’m gone. Or the fan in my office will click weird and suddenly I’m listening to that instead of reading what’s in front of me. Someone walks past my door and I’ll look up, and then somehow I’m opening another tab, checking something else, and the thing I was supposed to finish is just… abandoned.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I know it looks like I’m being careless, but it feels more like my attention just gets yanked away before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely high or irritable that lasted for at least a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There are some weeks where I feel almost electric, like I’m unstoppable and everything should move at my speed.\n\nAnd then it’s weird, because it doesn’t take much to flip it into irritation. Like if there’s a tiny delay, or my wife asks me a simple question, I’ll suddenly feel like she’s slowing me down on purpose. Which, I know, that sounds unfair when I say it out loud. But in the moment it feels like everybody is in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these high-energy periods come and go, or is it more like your mood and energy level are always fluctuating?\n\nPatient: It comes and goes, I think. It’s not like I’m exactly the same level all the time.\n\nThere are weeks where I feel almost electric, like I can do anything and I don’t understand why everyone else is moving so slowly. And then—this is the part I don’t like—something really small, like a delay on a project or my wife just asking me a normal question, can make me snap. In my head it feels like people are slowing me down on purpose.\n\nWhich, I mean, I know that sounds kind of ridiculous sitting here saying it. It’s not like she’s doing anything wrong. But when I’m in that state, it doesn’t feel small. It feels like everything is in my way.\n\nDoctor: Do you consider these high-energy periods to be separate episodes, or do they blend together over time?\n\nPatient: They feel more like separate stretches, I guess—not one constant thing. Like I’ll have these runs where I’m unusually driven and sleeping less, and I feel way too sure of myself.\n\nIt’s been happening… maybe for about a year and eight months now? I remember trying to pin it down because it started affecting work, and then I got distracted thinking it was just the job getting more intense, because in tech everything is always “urgent,” you know? But no, it’s not just the job.\n\nSo yeah, they come in stretches, but over time it’s all kind of started to blur together a little.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always coming down from one high-energy period just to go into another, or are there clear breaks in between?\n\nPatient: There are breaks, I think… but sometimes it feels like I’m just cycling through them, like I finally come down and then some new push starts up again.\n\nThe bigger thing is, those unusually driven stretches—the not sleeping much, feeling overconfident, like I’m operating at a higher speed—those have been part of my life for… about a year and eight months now, roughly. I remember trying to count it out and then getting sidetracked because work has its own crazy rhythm, and it’s hard to tell what’s me and what’s just the job. A tiny delay at work, or my wife asking me a totally normal question, can make me snap. In the moment it feels like people are slowing me down on purpose, even though later I can see that doesn’t really make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always coming down from one high-energy period just to go into another?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, it kind of does feel that way. There are breaks, but they don’t feel like they last as long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these high-energy periods have a clear beginning and end, or do they kind of blend together?\n\nPatient: They kind of blend together, I think. Like if my wife asks one question or something takes five minutes longer than I expected, I can snap at it like people are deliberately trying to slow me down. Even if, later, I know that’s probably not what was happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I’ll be trying to finish a report or close out a ticket, and then my phone buzzes, or the fan makes this little clicking sound, or someone just walks past my office door, and my attention just snaps over there.\n\nAnd then somehow I’m not doing the thing anymore. I’ll be checking the message, or thinking about whether the fan needs replacing—actually I had this one fan in my office that kept doing that, and I spent way too long looking up quieter ones—anyway, yeah, it’s hard to stay with one thing for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that your mood changes rapidly and unexpectedly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty often. It can come on really fast, like I’ll be feeling… I don’t know, charged up, almost electric, like I can handle everything and nothing can touch me.\n\nAnd then something small happens—my wife asks a simple question, or there’s a little delay with something—and I just snap. In the moment it feels like people are slowing me down on purpose, even if later I can see that maybe they weren’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt extremely high or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah. There are stretches where I’ll sleep maybe three hours, and then I’m just… up. Like wide awake at 3 or 4 in the morning, not groggy, not thinking, “Oh no, I need more sleep.”\n\nIt feels more like someone gave me extra hours in the day. I’ll be lying there thinking about all the stuff I can get done, and then I’m already up before the alarm. Which sounds useful, I guess, except it’s not really normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt extremely high or irritable that lasted for several days or longer?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it can last more than just a bad afternoon. Some weeks I get this almost electric feeling, like I’m unstoppable and everything should move at my speed.\n\nBut then the smallest thing can flip it. Like if something takes an extra minute, or my wife asks me a normal question, I’ll just snap, like—why are you trying to slow me down? And I know that sounds ridiculous when I say it sitting here, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these mood swings and periods of high energy?\n\nPatient: I think… about a year and eight months, roughly. Not like I wrote it down on a calendar, but that’s when I started noticing these stretches where I was way more driven, barely sleeping, and feeling like I knew better than everyone.\n\nIt’s weird, because at first I kind of told myself it was just work stress or getting into a productive groove—my job has these crunch periods, and everybody talks about “grinding” like it’s normal. But looking back, yeah, the high-energy, overconfident stuff has been going on for around a year and eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever spent money recklessly or made impulsive purchases during these high-energy periods?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, my wife would say yes, probably faster than I would.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m buying sports cars or something. Sometimes it’s “work stuff,” you know? Gear, subscriptions, things I convinced myself were necessary because I was going to fix everything or build something better overnight. And then it kind of blends into the bigger mess.\n\nAfter that migration failed at work, I got put on leave, and at home my wife had to start stepping in more—watching the spending, telling me to stop working at midnight, trying to keep normal conversations from turning into arguments. I hate saying that because it makes me sound like a child.\n\nAnd I’ve pulled back from other things too—church music, climbing, mentoring people—because I can’t reliably show up or follow through. So… yeah, the spending is part of it, but it’s more like the whole thing spills over everywhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you return to a normal state between these high-energy periods?\n\nPatient: Sort of… I mean, I do come down from that wired, speeding feeling. There are stretches where I’m not especially “up,” and I’m not really depressed either—just kind of calm, or flat, I guess. Sometimes that lasts a few weeks.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d call it completely normal, because even during those quieter times, I’ll still get stuck on things. Like Teams statuses at work—I know it sounds stupid—but I’ll feel like they’re coded warnings, like people are signaling something without saying it. And sometimes I’ll hear these quick little comments when nobody’s actually there.\n\nSo mood-wise, yeah, I can seem more normal between the high-energy periods. But it’s not like everything fully shuts off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt really down or depressed for a period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic, like I’m crying all day or anything. It’s more like I wake up and I already feel like this failed version of myself, like I’m behind before the day even starts. Heavy, flat. And then my wife will ask something normal—like what I want for dinner, or if I paid a bill—and it feels like too much to even answer, which makes no sense because it’s just a question.\n\nI don’t know… sometimes I’ll sit there thinking I should be able to handle basic things. I manage systems for a living, or I’m supposed to, and then at home I can’t even have a simple conversation without feeling buried by it. So yes, there are definitely periods where I feel really down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things that wouldn't normally distract other people?\n\nPatient: Yeah, very much. Especially when I’m revved up.\n\nLike, I’ll be trying to finish a report or respond to a ticket, and if my phone buzzes—even once—I’m gone. Or the fan in my office will click weird and suddenly I’m listening to that instead of reading what’s in front of me. Someone walks past my door and I’ll look up, and then somehow I’m opening another tab, checking something else, and the thing I was supposed to finish is just… abandoned.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I know it looks like I’m being careless, but it feels more like my attention just gets yanked away before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely high or irritable that lasted for at least a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There are some weeks where I feel almost electric, like I’m unstoppable and everything should move at my speed.\n\nAnd then it’s weird, because it doesn’t take much to flip it into irritation. Like if there’s a tiny delay, or my wife asks me a simple question, I’ll suddenly feel like she’s slowing me down on purpose. Which, I know, that sounds unfair when I say it out loud. But in the moment it feels like everybody is in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these high-energy periods come and go, or is it more like your mood and energy level are always fluctuating?\n\nPatient: It comes and goes, I think. It’s not like I’m exactly the same level all the time.\n\nThere are weeks where I feel almost electric, like I can do anything and I don’t understand why everyone else is moving so slowly. And then—this is the part I don’t like—something really small, like a delay on a project or my wife just asking me a normal question, can make me snap. In my head it feels like people are slowing me down on purpose.\n\nWhich, I mean, I know that sounds kind of ridiculous sitting here saying it. It’s not like she’s doing anything wrong. But when I’m in that state, it doesn’t feel small. It feels like everything is in my way.\n\nDoctor: Do you consider these high-energy periods to be separate episodes, or do they blend together over time?\n\nPatient: They feel more like separate stretches, I guess—not one constant thing. Like I’ll have these runs where I’m unusually driven and sleeping less, and I feel way too sure of myself.\n\nIt’s been happening… maybe for about a year and eight months now? I remember trying to pin it down because it started affecting work, and then I got distracted thinking it was just the job getting more intense, because in tech everything is always “urgent,” you know? But no, it’s not just the job.\n\nSo yeah, they come in stretches, but over time it’s all kind of started to blur together a little.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always coming down from one high-energy period just to go into another, or are there clear breaks in between?\n\nPatient: There are breaks, I think… but sometimes it feels like I’m just cycling through them, like I finally come down and then some new push starts up again.\n\nThe bigger thing is, those unusually driven stretches—the not sleeping much, feeling overconfident, like I’m operating at a higher speed—those have been part of my life for… about a year and eight months now, roughly. I remember trying to count it out and then getting sidetracked because work has its own crazy rhythm, and it’s hard to tell what’s me and what’s just the job. But it’s been around that long.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually return to a completely normal mood between these high-energy periods or do you feel like you're always somewhat elevated?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it completely normal. Like, there are times I’m not as revved up, but then some weeks I feel almost… electric, like I’m unstoppable, and everything is moving too slow around me.\n\nAnd then it’s weird, because it’s not just “happy” or productive. A tiny delay at work, or my wife asking me a totally normal question, can make me snap. In the moment it feels like people are slowing me down on purpose, even though later I can see that doesn’t really make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always coming down from one high-energy period just to go into another?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, it kind of does feel that way. Like the busier, pressured, high-energy stretches have taken up more space over the last couple years than the quieter times.\n\nI mean, I try to think of it in terms of work projects—because that’s how I remember time, like “was that before the migration or after,” that kind of thing—and when I do that, it seems like there were more stretches where I was keyed up than stretches where I was really just settled. There are breaks, but they don’t feel like they last as long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these high-energy periods have a clear beginning and end, or do they kind of blend together?\n\nPatient: They kind of blend together, I think. I can point to some stretches that felt like they started with a project or a deadline, but looking back, the high-energy, pressured times have taken up more of the last couple years than the quieter periods.\n\nIt’s like… I’ll think, “Okay, that was just a busy month,” but then there’s another busy month after it, and another thing I’m pushing through, and the calm parts feel shorter when I try to remember it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always in some phase of a high-energy period, or are there times when you feel completely separate from those episodes?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s hard to say I’m ever completely separate from it, if that makes sense. Looking back, the high-energy, pressured parts have kind of taken up more space over the last couple years than the quieter in-between times.\n\nLike, I’ll tell myself, “Oh, that was just because work was intense,” and then I start thinking about a server rollout or a budget meeting or whatever, and I get off track, but when I line it all up… the calmer stretches just seem shorter. It feels like I’m usually in some version of that revved-up mode, or not that far from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always in a high-energy state, or are there distinct episodes of high energy followed by periods of normal or low energy?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s one constant high all the time, exactly… but when I look back over the last couple years, the high-energy, pressured stretches seem to take up more room than the normal or low periods.\n\nLike, I can tell myself, “Oh, that was just that work push,” and then I remember another thing right after it — some meeting cycle or system issue — and I sort of lose track. But overall, the quieter in-between times feel shorter than the keyed-up ones.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
